That's what's been happening.\n\nDoctor: Has this tendency to jump between tasks been something you have dealt with since you were a child, or is this a more recent development?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose it's not exactly new. It goes back to... well, childhood. Even back in grade school, I know my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright, but... scattered. I guess I was always losing my papers, or... talking out of turn. Wiggling in my seat before I'd actually finished my schoolwork. So... yes. It's been a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with staying on task and sitting still cause significant problems for you in your daily life, such as at work or in your social relationships?\n\nPatient: ...Well, yes, I suppose it does cause some trouble. It's... it's been a bit of a strain lately. Mostly at home, and... down at the community center. So, yes, it does cause some problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulty with sitting still and your tendency to talk out of turn are more disruptive to your daily life than your tendency to lose your belongings or leave tasks unfinished?\n\nPatient: ...I'm not really sure. I suppose... they all just sort of blend together. I haven't really thought about which one is worse. It's just... how things are.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, would you say that your constant need to move or your difficulty waiting for your turn affects your daily life more than your tendency to lose things or leave tasks unfinished?\n\nPatient: ...I think I just said I wasn't sure. It's hard to separate them out like that. I don't really know which is worse... they just both happen.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your childhood, would you say that being physically restless or needing to be 'on the go' was more noticeable to others than your difficulty with staying focused on a single task?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose they were both pretty noticeable. I remember... back in grade school, the teachers would tell my mother that I was bright but... scattered. They said I was always losing my papers and talking out of turn. And... wiggling in my seat instead of finishing my work. So... I guess they were both there, even back then.\n\nDoctor: If you had to look at your life today, would you say that your physical restlessness and tendency to act or speak without thinking are more frequent and intense than your tendency to lose focus or leave projects unfinished?\n\nPatient: ...I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, it's all just... part of my day. I don't sit around measuring which one happens more often. They just... happen.\n\nDoctor: If you compare your day-to-day life now to your childhood, would you say that your need to be on the go and your tendency to act or speak without thinking have become less of a problem for you than your difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: ...I don't know. It's hard to compare now to when I was a little girl. I'm 70 years old, so... my life is just very different now. I'm not in school, I'm not working. I just... I don't really know if one is less of a problem than the other. They're just... still there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's just... how things are.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, would you say that your constant need to move or your difficulty waiting for your turn affects your daily life more than your tendency to lose things or leave tasks unfinished?\n\nPatient: ...I think I just said I wasn't sure. It's hard to separate them out like that. I don't really know which is worse... they just both happen.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your childhood, would you say that being physically restless or needing to be 'on the go' was more noticeable to others than your difficulty with staying focused on a single task?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose they were both pretty noticeable. I remember... back in grade school, the teachers would tell my mother that I was bright but... scattered. They said I was always losing my papers and talking out of turn. And... wiggling in my seat instead of finishing my work. So... I guess they were both there, even back then.\n\nDoctor: If you had to look at your life today, would you say that your physical restlessness and tendency to act or speak without thinking are more frequent and intense than your tendency to lose focus or leave projects unfinished?\n\nPatient: ...I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, it's all just... part of my day. I don't sit around measuring which one happens more often. They just... happen.\n\nDoctor: If you compare your day-to-day life now to your childhood, would you say that your need to be on the go and your tendency to act or speak without thinking have become less of a problem for you than your difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: ...I don't know. It's hard to compare now to when I was a little girl. I'm 70 years old, so... my life is just very different now. I'm not in school, I'm not working. I just... I don't really know if one is less of a problem than the other. They're just... still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you still frequently feel the need to move about or talk when others are quiet, even in settings where it might be expected that you sit still or remain silent?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose so. Quiet things are just... hard for me to keep quiet. Even if I'm just knitting, I usually end up chatting... or humming to myself. Or I'll keep getting up to look for supplies instead of just... sitting calmly. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: If you had to look at your daily life over the past six months, would you say that you struggle more with staying focused and organized, or with your physical restlessness and tendency to act or speak without thinking?\n\nPatient: ...I really can't say. It's just... like I said before, I don't measure these things. They both just happen. I haven't noticed one being worse than the other.\n\nDoctor: When you are at the community center, do you find that you struggle more with completing your assigned tasks, or with waiting for your turn to speak during meetings?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really think about it like that. I just... go and do what I'm supposed to do. I haven't really noticed if the meetings are harder than the tasks. It's just... a community center. I don't keep track of which one is worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you frequently make careless mistakes in your daily tasks because you have trouble paying close attention to the details?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose so. Sometimes when I'm helping out with the papers at the community center, I'll... miss a name on the sign-up sheet. Or I'll write down the wrong time for a room reservation on a note. I usually think I've checked it over fine, but... mistakes just slip through, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In your daily life, how often do you find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences before they have finished speaking?\n\nPatient: ...I don't know how often, exactly. My husband tells me I cut him off a lot. And... I guess I do it when we're planning things at the center, too. I just... if I don't say it right then, I'll forget what I was thinking. So I just say it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the last six months, would you say that you have experienced both a significant difficulty with staying focused on tasks and a persistent need to be physically active or talkative?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if I'd call it significant. I just... I go about my days. Sometimes my mind wanders off when I'm trying to get things done, and... I suppose I do talk a fair bit. But it's just how I've always been. I don't know if it's both of those things all the time.\n\nDoctor: When you are trying to focus on a task, do you often find that your physical restlessness or the urge to talk makes it even harder for you to stay on that task?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if they're connected like that. I mean... I do get fidgety, and I know I talk, but... I haven't really noticed if one makes the other worse. I just... lose track of what I'm doing sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

They're just... still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you still frequently feel the need to move about or talk when others are quiet, even in settings where it might be expected that you sit still or remain silent?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose so. Quiet things are just... hard for me to keep quiet. Even if I'm just knitting, I usually end up chatting... or humming to myself. Or I'll keep getting up to look for supplies instead of just... sitting calmly. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: If you had to look at your daily life over the past six months, would you say that you struggle more with staying focused and organized, or with your physical restlessness and tendency to act or speak without thinking?\n\nPatient: ...I really can't say. It's just... like I said before, I don't measure these things. They both just happen. I haven't noticed one being worse than the other.\n\nDoctor: When you are at the community center, do you find that you struggle more with completing your assigned tasks, or with waiting for your turn to speak during meetings?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really think about it like that. I just... go and do what I'm supposed to do. I haven't really noticed if the meetings are harder than the tasks. It's just... a community center. I don't keep track of which one is worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you frequently make careless mistakes in your daily tasks because you have trouble paying close attention to the details?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose so. Sometimes when I'm helping out with the papers at the community center, I'll... miss a name on the sign-up sheet. Or I'll write down the wrong time for a room reservation on a note. I usually think I've checked it over fine, but... mistakes just slip through, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In your daily life, how often do you find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences before they have finished speaking?\n\nPatient: ...I don't know how often, exactly. My husband tells me I cut him off a lot. And... I guess I do it when we're planning things at the center, too. I just... if I don't say it right then, I'll forget what I was thinking. So I just say it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the last six months, would you say that you have experienced both a significant difficulty with staying focused on tasks and a persistent need to be physically active or talkative?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if I'd call it significant. I just... I go about my days. Sometimes my mind wanders off when I'm trying to get things done, and... I suppose I do talk a fair bit. But it's just how I've always been. I don't know if it's both of those things all the time.\n\nDoctor: When you are trying to focus on a task, do you often find that your physical restlessness or the urge to talk makes it even harder for you to stay on that task?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if they're connected like that. I mean... I do get fidgety, and I know I talk, but... I haven't really noticed if one makes the other worse. I just... lose track of what I'm doing sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past six months, would you say that you have had at least six distinct symptoms of being easily distracted or disorganized, as well as at least six distinct symptoms of being physically restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: ...I really couldn't give you a number. Six of this, six of that... I don't keep count like that. I just live my life. I forget things sometimes, and I like to keep moving. I don't know if it's as many as you're asking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I haven't noticed one being worse than the other.\n\nDoctor: When you are at the community center, do you find that you struggle more with completing your assigned tasks, or with waiting for your turn to speak during meetings?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really think about it like that. I just... go and do what I'm supposed to do. I haven't really noticed if the meetings are harder than the tasks. It's just... a community center. I don't keep track of which one is worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you frequently make careless mistakes in your daily tasks because you have trouble paying close attention to the details?\n\nPatient: ...I suppose so. Sometimes when I'm helping out with the papers at the community center, I'll... miss a name on the sign-up sheet. Or I'll write down the wrong time for a room reservation on a note. I usually think I've checked it over fine, but... mistakes just slip through, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In your daily life, how often do you find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences before they have finished speaking?\n\nPatient: ...I don't know how often, exactly. My husband tells me I cut him off a lot. And... I guess I do it when we're planning things at the center, too. I just... if I don't say it right then, I'll forget what I was thinking. So I just say it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the last six months, would you say that you have experienced both a significant difficulty with staying focused on tasks and a persistent need to be physically active or talkative?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if I'd call it significant. I just... I go about my days. Sometimes my mind wanders off when I'm trying to get things done, and... I suppose I do talk a fair bit. But it's just how I've always been. I don't know if it's both of those things all the time.\n\nDoctor: When you are trying to focus on a task, do you often find that your physical restlessness or the urge to talk makes it even harder for you to stay on that task?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if they're connected like that. I mean... I do get fidgety, and I know I talk, but... I haven't really noticed if one makes the other worse. I just... lose track of what I'm doing sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past six months, would you say that you have had at least six distinct symptoms of being easily distracted or disorganized, as well as at least six distinct symptoms of being physically restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: ...I really couldn't give you a number. Six of this, six of that... I don't keep count like that. I just live my life. I forget things sometimes, and I like to keep moving. I don't know if it's as many as you're asking about.\n\nDoctor: If you compare your daily life over the last six months, would you say that you have more difficulty with your mind wandering and losing track of things, or with your physical restlessness and tendency to speak out of turn?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know which one is worse. I haven't really thought about it like a competition between the two. They just... both happen, I guess. I couldn't say if one gives me more trouble than the other.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I haven't noticed one being worse than the other.\n\nDoctor: When you are at the community center, do you find that you struggle more with completing your assigned tasks, or with waiting for your turn to speak during meetings?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really think about it like that. So I just say it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the last six months, would you say that you have experienced both a significant difficulty with staying focused on tasks and a persistent need to be physically active or talkative?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if I'd call it significant. I don't know if it's both of those things all the time.\n\nDoctor: When you are trying to focus on a task, do you often find that your physical restlessness or the urge to talk makes it even harder for you to stay on that task?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know if they're connected like that. I mean... I do get fidgety, and I know I talk, but... I haven't really noticed if one makes the other worse. I just... lose track of what I'm doing sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past six months, would you say that you have had at least six distinct symptoms of being easily distracted or disorganized, as well as at least six distinct symptoms of being physically restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: ...I really couldn't give you a number. I don't know if it's as many as you're asking about.\n\nDoctor: If you compare your daily life over the last six months, would you say that you have more difficulty with your mind wandering and losing track of things, or with your physical restlessness and tendency to speak out of turn?\n\nPatient: ...I don't really know which one is worse.
